Quantum Grothendieck ring isomorphisms, cluster algebras and Kazhdan-Lusztig
algorithm
David Hernandez , Hironori Oya Show more
View PDF Download full issue Add to Mendeley 10.1016/j.aim.2019.02.024
Abstract
We establish ring isomorphisms between quantum Grothendieck rings of certain
remarkable monoidal categories  and  of finite-dimensional
representations of quantum affine algebras of types  and , respectively. Our
proof relies in part on the corresponding quantum cluster algebra structures.
Moreover, we prove that our isomorphisms specialize at  to the isomorphisms of
(classical) Grothendieck rings obtained recently by Kashiwara, Kim and Oh by other
methods. As a consequence, we prove a conjecture formulated by the first author in
2002: the multiplicities of simple modules in standard modules in  are given by
the specialization of certain analogues of Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials and the
coefficients of these polynomials are positive.
Keywords
Quantum affine algebras; Quantum Grothendieck rings; Kazhdan-Lusztig algorithm;
T-systems; Quantum cluster algebras; Dual canonical bases
Previous article in this issue Next article in this issue
1. Introduction
Cluster algebras can be realized in terms of various important monoidal categories,
such as finite-dimensional representations of quantum affine algebras, perverse
sheaves on quiver varieties, representations of quiver-Hecke algebras, equivariant
perverse coherent sheaves on the affine Grassmannian... (see the very recent [6] and
[28] for a review).
In this paper, inspired by this general framework of monoidal categorifications, we
get new results on the representation theory of quantum affine algebras and on the
positivity of certain corresponding Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials.

===== PAGE 2 =====
Let 𝑈𝑞( ˆ𝑔) be an untwisted quantum affine algebra of quantum parameter 𝑞 ∈ 𝐶×
which is not a root of unity. Consider 𝐶 to be the monoidal category of finite-
dimensional representations of the algebra 𝑈𝑞( ˆ𝑔) (those representations are also
regarded as representations of a quantum loop algebra 𝑈𝑞(𝐿𝑔)). This category has
been studied from several geometric, algebraic, combinatorial perspectives in
connections to various fields, see [51], [38], [17] for important recent developments
and [11] for a very recent point of view in the context of physics.
The category 𝐶 is non-semisimple, and non-braided. It has a very intricate structure
and many basic questions are still open. The simple objects in 𝐶 have been classified
by Chari-Pressley in terms of Drinfeld polynomials [8], but the dimensions and
characters of these simple objects are not known in general.
The fundamental modules in 𝐶 are distinguished simple modules whose classes
generate its Grothendieck ring 𝐾(𝐶). (We do not distinguish the term “module” from
“representation” in this paper.) For simply-laced types, Nakajima established in [54] a
remarkable Kazhdan-Lusztig algorithm to compute the multiplicity 𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′ of a simple
module 𝐿(𝑚′) in a standard module 𝑀(𝑚), that is, a tensor product of fundamental
modules. Here m, 𝑚′ belong to an ordered set of monomials 𝑀 which parametrizes
both simple and standard objects. This allows one to calculate the classes of simple
modules [𝐿(𝑚)] ∈ 𝐾(𝐶) in terms of the classes of fundamental modules. As the
dimensions and characters of the latter are known, this gives an answer to the
problem of calculating the dimensions and characters of arbitrary simple objects: the
multiplicities 𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′ are proved to be the evaluation at 𝑡 = 1 of analogues 𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′(𝑡) of
Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials. These polynomials are constructed from the structure
of the quantum Grothendieck ring 𝐾𝑡(𝐶), which is a t-deformation of 𝐾(𝐶) in a certain
quantum torus [54], [60]. Besides it is proved that the coefficients of the polynomials
𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′(𝑡) are positive, as well as the structure constants of the quantum Grothendieck
ring 𝐾𝑡(𝐶) on a canonical basis whose elements are called (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of simple
modules. These results are based on the geometric construction of standard modules
in terms of quiver varieties known only for simply-laced types (note however that
geometric character formulas for standard modules have been obtained in [31] for all
types).
The existence of an algorithm to compute the multiplicity of a simple module in a
standard module is still not known in the case of non-simply-laced untwisted
quantum affine algebras. A conjectural answer was proposed by the first author in
[23] by giving another construction of the quantum Grothendieck ring 𝐾𝑡(𝐶) and the
corresponding polynomials 𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′(𝑡) which can be extended to general types. This
leads to a general precise conjectural formula for the multiplicity of simple modules
in standard modules
where < is the partial ordering on monomials as above. Besides it is not clear from
[23] that the polynomials 𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′(𝑡) have positive coefficients (although it has been
checked in numerous cases by computer). As far as the authors know, these two
independent conjectures, (1.1) and positivity, are completely open at the moment. In
the present paper, we establish these conjectures for certain remarkable monoidal
categories in the case of type B𝑛
(1).

𝑚′ < 𝑚
𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′(1)[𝐿(𝑚′)], (1.1)

For a simply-laced type X𝑛
(1), the category 𝐶 has interesting monoidal subcategories
𝐶𝑄, X𝑛 introduced in [30] depending on a quiver 𝑄 whose underlying graph is the
Dynkin diagram of type X𝑛. This monoidal subcategory 𝐶𝑄, X𝑛 is sometimes called a
Hernandez-Leclerc subcategory. It is proved in [30] that the quantum Grothendieck
ring 𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, X𝑛) is isomorphic to the quantized coordinate algebra 𝐴𝑡−1[𝑁−X𝑛] of type X𝑛
(it was recently established [16] that certain completions of these categories have a
structure of affine highest weight category). For type B𝑛
(1), analogues 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛 of the
categories 𝐶𝑄, X𝑛 were defined in [57]. In this paper, we establish an analogue of the
isomorphism in [30] for 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛:
(Theorem 10.1, Corollary 10.2). One notable point about this isomorphism is that the
quantized coordinate algebra side is of type A2𝑛 − 1, and not of type B𝑛.
In the proof of (1.2), we use the quantum cluster structure of the quantized
coordinate algebras as follows. We first show the isomorphism between ambient
quantum tori of 𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛) and 𝐴𝑡−1[𝑁−A2𝑛 − 1]. The former arises from the definition of
(truncated) (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters and the latter comes from the quantum cluster algebra
structure of 𝐴𝑡−1[𝑁−A2𝑛 − 1]. Then we prove the correspondence between distinguished
systems of relations: the quantum T-system of type B that we establish in 𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛)
(Theorem 9.6) and some specific mutation sequence in the quantum cluster algebra
structure. This strategy is partially in line with the one in [30] aside from some
technical differences. For example, we do not know a priori some positivity properties
of (truncated) (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters for type B𝑛
(1).
As an immediate corollary of (1.2), we can say that 𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛) has a quantum cluster
algebra structure coming from that of 𝐴𝑡−1[𝑁−A2𝑛 − 1]. Then the initial quiver for this
quantum cluster algebra structure is nothing but a subquiver of the quiver introduced
by the first author and Leclerc [31] which is defined for a subcategory of the category
𝐶 of type B𝑛
(1). See Remark 6.2, Remark 10.3.
We also show that the isomorphism (1.2) maps a basis {𝐿𝑡(𝑚)}𝑚 of 𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛)
parametrized by simple modules 𝐿(𝑚) in 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛 (called the (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of the
simple modules 𝐿(𝑚) as above) to the dual canonical basis of the quantized
coordinate algebra in the sense of Lusztig and Kashiwara. This implies the positivity
property conjectured in [23] for the category 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛:
(P1) The positivity of the coefficients of the polynomials 𝑃𝑚, 𝑚′(𝑡).
As a by-product we also establish two additional positivity properties:
(P2) The positivity of structure constants of the quantum Grothendieck ring
𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛).
(P3) The positivity of the coefficients of the truncated (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of 𝐿(𝑚)
.

Combining (1.3) with our isomorphism (1.2), we obtain an isomorphism between
𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛) ≃ 𝐴𝑡−1[𝑁−A2𝑛 − 1] (1.2)
𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, A2𝑛 − 1) ≃ 𝐴𝑡−1[𝑁−A2𝑛 − 1] . (1.3)

Here we should note that 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛 is determined by the Dynkin quiver 𝑄′ of type A2𝑛 − 2,
which is irrelevant to the A2𝑛 − 1-quiver 𝑄, and some additional data ♭ ∈ { > , < }. See
section 3 for details (the category 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛 above is denoted by 𝐶
(𝑄′)
♭ there). Since the
isomorphism (1.3) also restricts to a bijection between the basis consisting of the
(𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of simple modules and the dual canonical basis, the isomorphism
(1.4) induces a bijection between the (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of simple modules in 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛 and
𝐶𝑄, A2𝑛 − 1. See also Remark 12.3.
We note that similarities between finite-dimensional modules of type A and B
quantum affine algebras had been observed in [26]. The Kirillov-Reshetikhin modules
are certain simple objects in 𝐶 generalizing fundamental modules. It is proved they
are thin in types A and B, that is, they have multiplicity-free Frenkel-Reshetikhin q-
characters [15], and that these q-characters have analogous explicit formulas in both
types (certain (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of Kirillov-Reshetikhin modules are used in the
construction of 𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛)).
To prove that the elements {𝐿𝑡(𝑚)}𝑚 specialize to the classes of simple modules as
𝑡 → 1, and to establish the formula (1.1) in 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛, we combine our results with those of
Kashiwara, Kim and Oh [43], [42]. These authors used different tools as in the present
paper, namely generalized quantum affine Schur-Weyl duality [33], to construct an
isomorphism 𝐾(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛) ≃ 𝐾(𝐶𝑄, A2𝑛 − 1) between classical Grothendieck rings. Moreover
they proved their isomorphism preserves the basis of simple modules. We show that
our isomorphism (1.4) of quantum Grothendieck rings specializes at 𝑡 = 1 to their
isomorphism (note that, as far as the authors know, the isomorphism (1.4) can not be
deduced directly from the results of the papers [43], [42]). This coincidence implies
for the category 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛 that the (𝑞, 𝑡)-character 𝐿𝑡(𝑚) specializes to the q-character of
𝐿(𝑚) as conjectured by the first author in [23]. In particular the conjectural formula
(1.1) holds for this category.
We obtain also the explicit correspondence of (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of simple modules
under (1.4) for special quivers. This is computed from the formulas for the change of
the Lusztig parametrizations of the dual canonical basis associated with a change of
reduced words. We observe that (1.4) does not preserve any of the classes of modules
one might naturally expect (e.g. fundamental or Kirillov-Reshetikhin modules). See
Example 12.11, Example 12.12. When the authors started to work on this
correspondence, the paper [42] of Kashiwara, Kim and Oh appeared, and they also
compute such correspondence in a different way. Our correspondence from type
A2𝑛 − 1
(1)  to type B𝑛
(1) coincides with the correspondence in [42, section 3] up to shift of
spectral parameters when we restrict it to 𝐶𝑄, A2𝑛 − 1. We note that we also describe the
correspondence from type B𝑛
(1) to type A2𝑛 − 1
(1)  in this paper, which seems not to be
written in [43] and [42].
Some parts of the proof in this paper, in particular for the quantum T-systems, rely on
the thinness of Kirillov-Reshetikhin modules discussed above. Although this property
is not satisfied in general, it is satisfied by fundamental modules in types C and G2
[26], which gives hope to get analogous results in these situations. We plan to come
back to this in another paper.

===== PAGE 5 =====
We expect that we could also study the whole category 𝐶 of type B𝑛
(1) by “gluing”
(infinitely many) copies of 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛 together (cf. [30, subsection 1.5, sections 7, 8]). For
example, we hope that we could describe a structure of 𝐾𝑡(𝐶) by revealing how the
copies of 𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, B𝑛) are “patched” in 𝐾𝑡(𝐶). In simply-laced cases, a “patching” of
𝐾𝑡(𝐶𝑄, X𝑛) was calculated in a uniform manner in [30] by means of derived Hall
algebras, whose analogues for non-simply-laced cases are not known now. We also
plan to discuss the whole category 𝐶 in a future paper.
Moreover, the meaning of the quantum Grothendieck ring 𝐾𝑡(𝐶) in terms of the
structure of the category 𝐶 has not been clarified in non-simply-laced cases yet.
Hence it is not known at the moment whether the isomorphism (1.4) arises as an
equivalence of categories, but it is certainly a question we would like to address.
The paper is organized as follows. In section 2, we briefly recall the definitions of
quantum loop algebras and their finite-dimensional representation theory. In section
3, we explain the definition of the remarkable monoidal categories considered in this
paper following [30] and [57]. Here we also show the important convexity of twisted
Auslander-Reiten quivers (Lemma 3.22). In section 4, we recall the definition of
quantum tori which will be ambient spaces of (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters. The important data
are the inverses of quantum Cartan matrices. Their explicit form for type B is
provided in Example 4.2, Example 4.3, and its calculation is explained in Appendix A.
In section 5, we explain another kind of quantum tori, that is, the quantum tori
arising from the quantum cluster algebra structures of quantized coordinate algebras.
In section 6, we prove the isomorphism between these two kinds of quantum tori as
explained just after (1.2). In section 7, we recall the definition of the quantum
Grothendieck rings and the (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of standard modules and simple
modules. In section 8, we briefly recall the basics of quantized coordinate algebras
including (normalized) unipotent quantum minors, quantum determinantal
identities, (normalized) dual Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt type bases and dual canonical
bases. In section 9, we prove the quantum T-system for type B𝑛
(1) relying on a feature
of type B𝑛(1): the thinness of Kirillov-Reshetikhin modules. In section 10, we prove
the isomorphism (1.2) together with the correspondence between (𝑞, 𝑡)-characters of
simple modules and dual canonical basis elements. In section 11, we give the proof of
the positivities (P1)–(P3) and explain the coincidence between our isomorphism and
the isomorphism established by Kashiwara and Oh [43], which implies (1.1) in 𝐶𝑄, B𝑛.
In section 12, we provide the explicit correspondence of simple modules under (1.4)
for some explicit quivers.

2. Quantum loop algebras and their representation theory
In this section, we give reminders on quantum loop algebras, their finite-dimensional
modules and the corresponding q-character theory.
2.1. Quantum loop algebras
Let 𝑔 be a complex simple Lie algebra of type X𝑁 (X𝑁 = A𝑁, B𝑁, C𝑁, … , G2), and ℎ be a
Cartan subalgebra of 𝑔. Set 𝐼: = {1, … , 𝑁} and let {𝛼𝑖 | 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼} (resp. {ℎ𝑖 | 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼}) be the set
of simple roots (resp. coroots), Δ+ the set of positive roots. Let
𝐶 = (𝑐𝑖𝑗)𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝐼 = ( 〈 ℎ𝑖, 𝛼𝑗 〉 )𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝐼 be the Cartan matrix of 𝑔. We follow the labelling in
[32, §4.8]. Write 𝑄: = ∑𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 𝑍𝛼𝑖 ⊂ ℎ⁎, called the root lattice of 𝑔, and set
𝑃: = {𝜆 ∈ ℎ⁎ | 〈 ℎ𝑖, 𝜆 〉 ∈ 𝑍 for all 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼}, called the weight lattice of 𝑔. For 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼, define
𝜛𝑖 ∈ 𝑃 as the element such that 〈 ℎ𝑗, 𝜛𝑖 〉 = 𝛿𝑖𝑗 for 𝑗 ∈ 𝐼, which is called an
fundamental weight. Set 𝑃+: = ∑𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 𝑍 ≥ 0𝜛𝑖, 𝑄+: = ∑𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 𝑍 ≥ 0𝛼𝑖, and
𝜌: = ∑𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 𝜛𝑖 ∈ 𝑃+. We define a partial order ≤ on P by 𝛽 ≤ 𝛽′ if and only if 𝛽′ − 𝛽 ∈ 𝑄+.

The quantum loop algebra is a quotient of the corresponding quantum affine algebra
and has a Hopf algebra structure which is defined via another realization of 𝑈𝑞(𝐿𝑔)
(called the Drinfeld-Jimbo presentation). See, for example, [7] for more details.

2.3. q-characters
The q-character, introduced by Frenkel and Reshetikhin [15], is an important tool for
the study of finite-dimensional modules over quantum loop algebras. In this
subsection, we briefly recall their definition and properties. See, for example, [7] for
the survey of this topic.

3. The monoidal subcategories associated with quivers
In [30], the first author and Leclerc introduced specific monoidal subcategories 𝐶𝑄 of
𝐶• in the case that the quantum loop algebra is of type X𝑁
(1) with X = A, D or E. These
subcategories are constructed from a Dynkin quiver 𝑄 of type X𝑁. In particular, the
essential datum is the Auslander-Reiten quiver 𝛤𝑄 associated with 𝑄. It is known that
the subcategory 𝐶𝑄 has interesting properties. For example, its complexified
Grothendieck ring is isomorphic to the coordinate algebra 𝐶[𝑁−] of the unipotent
group 𝑁− of type X𝑁, and the simple modules in 𝐶𝑄 categorify the dual canonical basis
of 𝐶[𝑁−] (see Theorem 12.1). Moreover it is a “heart” of the whole category (cf. [30,
subsection 1.5]). See subsection 3.2 for the definition of 𝐶𝑄, and see subsection 12.1
and [30], [16] for detailed properties.
In this paper, we deal also with an analogue of these subcategories in the case of type
B𝑛
(1), which is introduced by Oh and Suh [57]. To define these subcategories, we need

3.1. The adapted reduced words and Auslander-Reiten quivers
In this subsection, we assume that 𝑔 is of type X𝑁 with X = A, D, E. Let 𝑄 = (𝑄0 = 𝐼, 𝑄1)
be a Dynkin quiver of type X𝑁, where 𝑄0 is the set of vertices (identified with the
index set I of simple roots) and 𝑄1 is the set of arrows. If there is an arrow from i to j
in 𝑄, then we write 𝑖 → 𝑗. A vertex 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 is called a sink (resp.a source) if there is no
arrow exiting out of (resp. entering into) i.
Let i be a sink or a source of 𝑄. Then define 𝑠𝑖(𝑄) as the quiver obtained from 𝑄 by
reversing the orientation of each arrow connected with i in 𝑄. A reduced word
𝑖 = (𝑖1, … , 𝑖ℓ) of an element w of W is said to be adapted to 𝑄 if
for all 𝑘 = 1, … , ℓ. There is a unique element 𝜙𝑄 ∈ 𝑊 such that
where {𝑖1, 𝑖2, … , 𝑖𝑁} = 𝐼, and (𝑖1, 𝑖2, … , 𝑖𝑁) is adapted to 𝑄. The element 𝜙𝑄 is called the
Coxeter element associated with 𝑄.
Now we shall recall the combinatorial construction [22], [30] of the Auslander-Reiten
quiver Γ𝑄 of the category of finite-dimensional modules over the path algebra 𝐶𝑄. By
definition, the vertex set of Γ𝑄 is the set of isomorphism classes of indecomposable
𝐶𝑄-modules. Gabriel's theorem states that this set is identified with the set Δ+ of
positive roots by taking their dimension vectors. Hence, we consider the vertex set of
Γ𝑄 as Δ+ in this paper. We do not review (in fact, do not need) here the details of the
representation theory of 𝐶𝑄. See, for instance, [1] for more details.
Fix a map 𝜉: 𝐼 → 𝑍 such that 𝜉(𝑗) = 𝜉(𝑖) + 1 whenever there exists an arrow 𝑖 → 𝑗 in 𝑄.
Such a map is called a height function associated with 𝑄. Set

3.2. The monoidal category 𝐶𝑄
Let 𝑄 be the Dynkin quiver of type X𝑁 with X = A, D, E, and ξ an associated height
function. As mentioned in the beginning of section 3, we provide the definition of the
monoidal subcategory 𝐶𝑄 of 𝐶• of type X𝑁
(1). In fact, we do not use 𝐶𝑄 until section 12,
but the definition of the nice monoidal subcategories in the case of type B𝑛
(1) is a
generalization of this construction (subsection 3.5). See subsection 12.1 and [30] for
properties of 𝐶𝑄.
Definition 3.5
[30, subsection 5.11]
Recall the labelling ˆ𝐼𝜉 of the positive roots Δ+ defined just after Proposition 3.1. Set
𝑌𝜉: = {𝑌𝑖, 𝑟 | (𝑖, 𝑟) ∈ ˆ𝐼𝜉} ⊂ 𝑌, and 𝐵𝜉 be the set of dominant monomials in the variables
in 𝑌𝜉.
Define 𝐶𝑄
𝜉  as the full subcategory of the category of 𝐶• of type X𝑁
(1) whose objects have
all their composition factors isomorphic to 𝐿(𝑚) for some 𝑚 ∈ 𝐵𝜉.
Remark 3.6
In the following, we write 𝐶𝑄
𝜉  simply as 𝐶𝑄. Note that the height function is uniquely
determined by the quiver 𝑄 up to shift, that is, if 𝜉′ is another height function
corresponding to 𝑄, then 𝜉 − 𝜉′ is a constant function. Hence the categories 𝐶𝑄
𝜉
 and 𝐶𝑄
𝜉′
are isomorphic via appropriate shift of the spectral parameter.
Remark 3.7
The monoidal subcategory 𝐶𝑄 corresponds to the monoidal subcategory 𝐶𝑄op in [30],
here 𝑄op is the Dynkin quiver obtained from 𝑄 by reversing the orientation of all
arrows. This difference arises from conventional difference of the definitions of
adaptedness and height functions.
By using convexity of Γ𝑄, we can prove the following:
Lemma 3.8
[30, Lemma 5.8]
The subcategory 𝐶𝑄 is closed under tensor product.

3.3. Combinatorial Auslander-Reiten quivers
The Auslander-Reiten quiver Γ𝑄, by definition, describes the category of finite-
dimensional modules over the path algebra 𝐶𝑄. Interestingly, it is known that Γ𝑄 also
visualizes a certain partial ordering on the set Δ+ of positive roots determined from 𝑄
(see Proposition 3.11 below). This point of view leads to a combinatorial
generalization of Auslander-Reiten quivers [56], which is an important tool in this
paper.
We consider the general setting in subsection 2.1 unless otherwise specified.
Definition 3.10
Two sequences i and 𝑖′ of elements of I are said to be commutation equivalent if i is
obtained from i by a sequence of transformations of adjacent components from (𝑖, 𝑗)
with (𝛼𝑖, 𝛼𝑗) = 0 into (𝑗, 𝑖). This is an equivalence relation, and the equivalence class of
i is called the commutation class of i, denoted by [𝑖].
Let 𝑤0 be the longest element of the Weyl group W. For 𝑖 = (𝑖1, 𝑖2, … , 𝑖ℓ) ∈ 𝐼(𝑤0), set
for 𝑘 = 1, … , ℓ. Then it is well-known that 𝛽𝑘
𝑖 ≠ 𝛽𝑘′
𝑖  if 𝑘 ≠ 𝑘′ and the set
{𝛽𝑘
𝑖 | 𝑘 = 1, … , ℓ} coincides with the set Δ+ of positive roots of 𝑔 [5]. For 𝑘 = 1, … , ℓ, 𝑖𝑘
is called the residue of 𝛽𝑘
𝑖 . Write
Note that the residues of the positive roots depend only on the commutation class of
i.
We consider the total ordering <𝑖  on Δ+ such that 𝛽𝑘

𝑖  if and only if 𝑘 < 𝑘′.
There is also the partial ordering ≺[𝑖]  on Δ+ associated with the commutation class
[𝑖] of i such that, for 𝛽, 𝛽′ ∈ Δ+, 𝛽 ≺[𝑖] 𝛽′ if and only if 𝛽 <𝑖′ 𝛽′ for all 𝑖′ ∈ [𝑖].
Proposition 3.11
[48, section 4], [59, section 6], [3, section 2]
Suppose that 𝑔 is of type X𝑁 with X = A, D, E. Let 𝑄 be a Dynkin quiver of type X𝑁. Then the
following hold:
(1) A reduced word 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼(𝑤0) is adapted to at most one Dynkin quiver 𝑄.
(2) For each Dynkin quiver 𝑄, there exists a reduced word 𝑖𝑄 of 𝑤0 adapted to 𝑄.
Moreover, all reduced words in its commutation class [𝑖𝑄] are adapted to 𝑄,
and all reduced words adapted to 𝑄 belong to [𝑖𝑄]. Hence this commutation
class is denoted by [𝑄].
(3) There exists 𝑖𝑐 = (𝑖1, 𝑖2 … , 𝑖ℓ) ∈ [𝑄] such that (𝑖1, 𝑖2, … , 𝑖𝑁) ∈ 𝐼(𝜙𝑄) (recall
(3.1)).
(4) For 𝛽, 𝛽′ ∈ 𝛥+, we have 𝛽 ≺[𝑄] 𝛽′ if and only if there is a path from 𝛽′ to β  in
𝛤𝑄.
(5) For 𝛽 ∈ 𝛥+ with 𝛺𝜉(𝛽) = (𝑖, 𝑟) (recall Proposition 3.1), we have res[𝑄](𝛽) = 𝑖.

===== PAGE 15 =====
Proposition 3.11 (4) states that the Auslander-Reiten quiver Γ𝑄 is regarded as a
visualization of the partial ordering ≺[𝑄] . A visualization of an arbitrary ≺[𝑖]  (of an
arbitrary type) is given by a combinatorial Auslander-Reiten quiver, introduced by Oh
and Suh [56]. The following is their explicit constructive definition:
Definition 3.12
[56, Algorithm 2.1]
Let 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼(𝑤0). The combinatorial Auslander-Reiten quiver ϒ[𝑖] associated with [𝑖] is
constructed as follows:
(i) the set of vertices consists of ℓ(𝑤0) elements labelled by Δ+.
(ii) there is an arrow from 𝛽𝑘
𝑖  to 𝛽𝑘′
𝑖 (see (3.2)) if and only if
In (ii), we use the reduced word i but it is easy to show that, even if we choose
another reduced word 𝑖′ commutation equivalent to i, we obtain the same quiver.
Hence the quiver ϒ[𝑖] depends only on the commutation class [𝑖] of i.
Remark 3.13
Oh and Suh considered the combinatorial Auslander-Reiten quivers associated with
(commutation classes of) reduced words of any element 𝑤 ∈ 𝑊 in the same manner.
Moreover, they considered colors of each arrow. However, we do not need them in
this paper.
The combinatorial Auslander-Reiten quivers defined above satisfy the desired
property:
Theorem 3.14
[56, Theorem 2.22, Theorem 2.29]
Two reduced words 𝑖, 𝑖′ ∈ 𝐼(𝑤0) are commutation equivalent if and only if ϒ[𝑖] = ϒ[𝑖′].
Moreover, for 𝛽, 𝛽′ ∈ 𝛥+, we have 𝛽 ≺[𝑖] 𝛽′ if and only if there is a path from 𝛽′ to β  in ϒ[𝑖].
A sequence (𝑗1, 𝑗2, … , 𝑗𝑙) is called a compatible reading of ϒ[𝑖] if
for a total ordering of its vertex set Δ+ = {𝛾1, 𝛾2, … , 𝛾ℓ} such that 𝑘′ < 𝑘 whenever
there is an arrow from 𝛾𝑘 to 𝛾𝑘′.
Theorem 3.15
[56, Theorem 2.23]
Every reduced expression of 𝑖′ in [𝑖] can be obtained by a compatible reading of ϒ[𝑖].
The following theorem guarantees that the combinatorial Auslander-Reiten quiver is
a combinatorial generalization of the usual Auslander-Reiten quiver.
(a) 𝑘 > 𝑘′ (b) (𝛼𝑖𝑘, 𝛼𝑖𝑘′) < 0 and (c) {𝑡 | 𝑘′ < 𝑡 < 𝑘, 𝑖𝑡 = 𝑖𝑘′ or 𝑖𝑘} = ∅ .
(𝑗1, 𝑗2, … , 𝑗𝑙) = (res[𝑖](𝛾1), res[𝑖](𝛾2), … , res[𝑖](𝛾ℓ)) (3.3)

Proof

Our definition of 𝐶𝑄♭ is apparently different from the original definition in [57,
Definition 9.2]. The following lemma guarantees that this subcategory coincides with
the one in [57]:
Lemma 3.26
The category 𝐶𝑄♭ is the smallest abelian full subcategory of 𝐶• of type B𝑛
(1) such that
(1) it is stable by taking subquotient, tensor product and extension,
(2) it contains the simple modules 𝐿(𝑌𝑖, 𝑟) with (𝑖, 𝑟) ∈ ‾𝐼𝜉
tw, ♭  and the trivial
module.
Proof
Let 𝐶′ be the full subcategory of 𝐶• defined as in the statement of the lemma. We shall
prove that 𝐶′ = 𝐶𝑄♭. By Proposition 2.5, any module 𝐿(𝑚) with 𝑚 ∈ 𝐵𝜉, ♭  belongs to 𝐶′.
Since 𝐶′ is closed under the extension, all objects in 𝐶𝑄♭ are contained in 𝐶′. Therefore,
we only have to check that 𝐶𝑄♭ satisfies (1) and (2) in the definition of 𝐶′.
The category 𝐶𝑄♭ is obviously stable by taking subquotient and extension, and
satisfies (2). Therefore it suffices to show that 𝐿(𝑚) ⊗ 𝐿(𝑚′) is an object of 𝐶𝑄♭ for all
𝑚, 𝑚′ ∈ 𝐵𝜉, ♭ .
Suppose that 𝐿(𝑚″) is a composition factor of 𝐿(𝑚) ⊗ 𝐿(𝑚′). Then 𝑚″ is a product of
monomials of 𝜒𝑞(𝐿(𝑚)) and 𝜒𝑞(𝐿(𝑚′)). Hence, by Theorem 2.6, we have 𝑚″ = 𝑚𝑚′𝑀
where M is a monomial in the variables 𝐴𝑖, 𝑟
−1. If M contains the term 𝐴𝑖, 𝑟
−1, then, by the
explicit form of 𝐴𝑖, 𝑟
−1 (cf. the left and right negativity of 𝐴𝑖, 𝑟
−1 [13]) and twisted convexity
of ϒ[𝑄♭] (the segment property), we have (𝑖, 𝑟 + 𝑟𝑖), (𝑖, 𝑟 − 𝑟𝑖) ∈ ‾𝐼𝜉
tw, ♭ . Hence it follows
from twisted convexity of ϒ[𝑄♭] again that 𝑚″ contains only the variables in 𝑌𝜉, ♭ ,
hence, 𝐿(𝑚) ⊗ 𝐿(𝑚′) is an object of 𝐶𝑄♭. □

4. Quantum tori
In the following, we deal with a t-deformed q-character homomorphism, called a
(𝑞, 𝑡)-character. First, we define a t-deformed version of 𝑌 = 𝑍[𝑌𝑖, 𝑟
±1 | 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼, 𝑟 ∈ 𝑍]
following [23]. The definition in [23] comes from the original construction of q-
characters in [15]: the variables 𝑌𝑖, 𝑝 are defined as formal power series that pairwise
commute. In [23], these formal power series are replaced by certain infinite sums
which can be seen as vertex operators and give rise to the t-commutative variables
(the parameter t itself appears as a formal power series). In [54], [60], a slightly
different quantum torus is defined for the simply-laced case from a convolution
operation for certain perverse sheaves on quiver varieties.

matrix of finite type. Define

5. Quantum tori for quantized coordinate algebras
We prepare the other quantum tori which arise from quantum cluster algebra
structures of quantized coordinate algebras [4]. Quantum cluster algebras are specific
subalgebras of skew field of fractions (and, a posteriori, subalgebras of quantum tori)
with infinitely many generators in general. It is known that many quantized
coordinate algebras in Lie theory have quantum cluster algebra structures. See [19],
[20], [21] and references therein.
In this section, we only recall the definitions concerning these quantum tori, and
review the quantum tori associated with quantum cluster algebra structures of some
quantized coordinate algebras. See Appendix B for the complete definition of
quantum cluster algebras.
5.1. Quantum tori in the theory of quantum cluster algebras

7. Quantum Grothendieck rings
In this section, we recall the definition of t-deformed q-characters, in other words, a
t-deformed Grothendieck ring 𝐾(𝐶•), following the approach in [23]. We should
remark that in the case of type A𝑁
(1), D𝑁
(1) and E𝑁
(1), such a t-deformed Grothendieck
ring was also constructed in [54], [60] by another method using quiver varieties (and
a slightly different quantum torus).

For 𝜆 ∈ 𝑃+ and 𝑤, 𝑤′ ∈ 𝑊, we can define a homogeneous element ˜𝐷𝑤𝜆, 𝑤′𝜆 of 𝐴𝑣[𝑁−],

Remark 8.1
There are several conventions of the definitions of quantized coordinate algebras and
(normalized) unipotent quantum minors. For example, in [30], quantized coordinate
algebras are defined through the positive half of the quantized enveloping algebras.
The reference [46, Remark 7.16] may be convenient to consider the relations between
several conventions. Note that our convention coincides with the one in [46] by just
adding 𝑣±1 / 2 in the base ring (see Appendix C.2 for the normalization of unipotent
quantum minors).
For 𝜆 ∈ 𝑃+ and 𝑤, 𝑤′ ∈ 𝑊, we have
The quantized coordinate algebra 𝐴𝑣[𝑁−] has a quantum cluster algebra structure
whose initial quantum cluster variables are described by the normalized unipotent
quantum minors:
Theorem 8.2
[19, Theorem 12.3], [21, Theorem 10.1]

Remark 11.2
The positivity of 𝑝𝑐, 𝑐′(𝑣) for type A𝑁, D𝑁 and E𝑁 was originally proved by Lusztig in his

through his geometric realization of the elements of the canonical bases and PBW-
type bases. However every reduced word in [𝑄♭] is non-adapted (Remark 3.20). In
[58, subsection 5.1], the second author provides a method for deducing this positivity
for arbitrary reduced words of 𝑤0 (when 𝑔 is of type A𝑁, D𝑁 and E𝑁) from Lusztig's
results.

12. Comparison between type A and B
In this section, we combine our results with the results of [30]. In [30], it is shown
that the category 𝐶𝑄 for type A2𝑛 − 1
(1)  (recall subsection 3.2) is also isomorphic to the
quantized coordinate algebra of A2𝑛 − 1. We recall the precise statement in subsection
12.1. Combining this result with Corollary 10.2, we obtain an isomorphism between
quantum Grothendieck rings of distinguished monoidal subcategories of 𝐶• of type
A2𝑛 − 1
(1)  and B𝑛
(1). In subsection 12.2, we give an explicit correspondence of the (𝑞, 𝑡)-
characters of simple modules under this isomorphism for special quivers (Theorem
12.9). This is computed from the well-known formulas for the change of the Lusztig
parametrizations (Definition 8.10) associated with a change of reduced words.

A quantum cluster pattern is an assignment of a quantum seed (𝑀𝑡, ˜𝐵𝑡) to every vertex
𝑡 ∈ 𝑇𝑒 such that 𝜇𝑘(𝑀𝑡, ˜𝐵𝑡) = (𝑀𝑡′, ˜𝐵𝑡′) whenever 𝑡 𝑘—𝑡′. Quantum seeds (𝑀, ˜𝐵𝑀),
(𝑀′, ˜𝐵𝑀′) are said to be mutation equivalent if there is a quantum cluster pattern such
that (𝑀𝑡, ˜𝐵𝑡) = (𝑀, ˜𝐵𝑀) and (𝑀𝑡′, ˜𝐵𝑡′) = (𝑀′, ˜𝐵𝑀′) for some 𝑡, 𝑡′ ∈ 𝑇𝑒.
Let 𝑆: = {(𝑀𝑡, ˜𝐵𝑡)}𝑡 ∈ 𝑇𝑒
 be a quantum cluster pattern in 𝐹. For 𝑡 ∈ 𝑇𝑒 and 𝑗 ∈ 𝐽, write
𝑋𝑗; 𝑡 = 𝑀𝑡(𝑒𝑗), and set 𝑋: = {𝑋𝑗; 𝑡 | 𝑡 ∈ 𝑇𝑒, 𝑗 ∈ 𝐽𝑒}. The elements of 𝑋 are called quantum
cluster variables. The set 𝑋𝑓: = {𝑀𝑡(𝑒𝑖) | 𝑖 ∈ 𝐽𝑓} does not depend on 𝑡 ∈ 𝑇𝑒. The
elements of 𝑋𝑓 are called frozen variables. A set of elements {𝑋𝑗; 𝑡 | 𝑗 ∈ 𝐽} is called the
cluster at t (the frozen variables are included in the clusters).
The quantum cluster algebra 𝐴(𝑆) is the 𝑍[𝑣±1 / 2]-subalgebra of 𝐹 generated by 𝑋 and
𝑋𝑓 (unlike [4], we do not add the inverses of the elements of 𝑋𝑓 in generators). If there
exists 𝑡0 ∈ 𝑇𝑒 such that 𝑀𝑡0(𝑐) = 𝑋𝑐 and ˜𝐵𝑡0 = ˜𝐵, then Λ𝑀𝑡0
= Λ and we write 𝐴(𝑆) as
𝐴(Λ, ˜𝐵). In this case, we have
for all 𝑡 ∈ 𝑇𝑒 and 𝑒 ∈ 𝑍𝐽 [4, Proposition 6.2].
Remark that 𝐴(𝑆) is isomorphic to 𝐴(Λ𝑀𝑡, ˜𝐵𝑡) for any 𝑡 ∈ 𝑇𝑒 in general.
